Formation of trapped surfaces in the Einstein-Yang-Mills system
Nikolaos Athanasiou, Puskar Mondal, Shing-Tung Yau

Abstract
We prove a scale-invariant, semi-global existence result and a trapped surface formation result in the context of coupled Einstein-Yang-Mills theory, without symmetry assumptions. More precisely, we prove a scale-invariant semi-global existence theorem from past null-infinity and show that the focusing of the gravitational and/or chromoelectric-chromomagnetic waves could lead to the formation of a trapped surface. Adopting the signature for decay rates approach introduced in [ 1 ] , we develop a novel gauge (and scale) invariant hierarchy of non-linear estimates for the Yang-Mills curvature which, together with the estimates for the gravitational degrees of freedom, yields the desired semi-global existence result. Once semi-global existence has been established, the formation of a trapped surface follows from a standard ODE argument.



1
Introduction

In the present work, we investigate the question of dynamical trapped surface formation for the Einstein-Yang-Mills system (E-Y-M system for short) for a
(3+1)-
dimensional Lorentzian manifold
\left(\mathcal{M},g\right)
and a (matrix-valued) Yang-Mills curvature two-form
{F^{P}}_{Q\alpha\beta}
(to be defined rigorously in Section
2.3
):

\displaystyle R_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}Rg_{\mu\nu}=\mathfrak{T}_{\mu\nu},

(1.1)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{T}_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}\left({F^{P}}_{Q\mu\alpha}{{F^{Q}}_{P\nu}}^{\alpha}+{\prescript{*}{}{F}^{P}}_{Q\mu\alpha}{{\prescript{*}{}{F}^{Q}}_{P\nu}}^{\alpha}\right),

(1.2)

where by
{\prescript{*}{}{F}^{P}}_{Q}
we denote the Hodge dual of
{F^{P}}_{Q}
.

1.1
Historical background

Trapped surfaces have been an object of significant interest within the classical theory of General Relativity for almost sixty years. After Schwarzschild’s discovery of his eponymous metric in 1915, it took almost twenty years before researchers came to realize the existence, within it, of a region
\mathcal{B}
with the following surprising yet salient features: First of all, observers situated inside
\mathcal{B}
cannot send signals to observers situated at an ideal
conformal boundary at infinity
, called
\mathcal{I}^{+}
.
Furthermore, any observer located inside
\mathcal{B}
lives only for finite proper time
1
1

1

Sbierski
[
15
]
moreover showed that the termination of the observer’s proper time manifests in a particularly ferocious way, as they, in fact, get torn apart by infinitely strong tidal forces.
(geodesic incompleteness).
The characteristics of this region (which later came to be known as a black hole) took most of the researchers of the time aback. The consensus seemed to be that these observed phenomena have to be accidents; pathologies, only present because of the strong (spherical) symmetry inherent in the Schwarzschild solution and that, in general solutions to the Einstein equations
2
2

2

The meaning of this phrase was not rigorous at the time, as the setup for the initial value problem in General Relativity had not yet been discovered.
, such phenomena would not arise. However, in the 60’s, this belief was spectacularly falsified by Roger Penrose through his celebrated incompleteness theorem
3
3

3

This theorem, in fact, was the main reason why he was awarded the Nobel Prize in Physics back in 2020, ”for the discovery that black hole formation is a robust prediction of the general theory of relativity”.
. It was Penrose
[
14
]
who introduced the notion of a
trapped suface
, without which one cannot state the incompleteness theorem :

Definition 1

Given a
(3+1)
- dimensional Lorentzian manifold
(\mathcal{M},g)
, a closed spacelike
2-
surface
S
is caled
trapped
if the following two fundamental forms
\chi
and
\underline{\chi}
have everywhere pointwise negative expansions on
S
:

\chi(X,Y):=g(D_{X}L,Y),\hskip 5.69054pt\underline{\chi}(X,Y):=g(D_{X}\underline{L},Y).

Here
D
denotes the Levi-Civita connection of
g
,
L
and
\underline{L}
denote a null basis of the 2-dimensional orthogonal complement of
T_{p}S
in
T_{p}\mathcal{M}
, extended as smooth vector fields and
X,Y
are arbitrary
S-
tangent vector fields.

In other words, a surface is called trapped if both
\mbox{tr}\chi
and
\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}
are pointwise negative everywhere on
S
. These traces signify the infinitesimal changes in area along the null generators normal to
S
, whence one can interpret trapped surfaces as closed, spacelike
2-
surfaces that infinitesimally decrease in area "along any possible future direction".

The incompleteness theorem is now presented.

Theorem 1.1
(Penrose Incompleteness)

Let
(\mathcal{M},g)
be a spacetime containing a non-compact Cauchy hypersurface. If
(\mathcal{M},g)
moreover satisfies the null energy codition and contains a closed trapped surface, it is geodesically incomplete.

The existence of a trapped surface is a stable feature in the context of dynamics. Indeed, sufficiently small perturbations of Schwarzschild initial data must also contain such surfaces, by Cauchy stability. As such, incompleteness is not an accident, but rather a recurring theme in the dynamics of the Einstein equations.

At the time, the existence of a trapped surface in a spacetime was too strong an assumption to begin with. In fact, the only way back then to guarantee its existence was to assume it at the level of initial data, but this itself can be a highly non-trivial question. The first trapped surface formation result at the level of initial data was given
in
[
16
]
. This however, begged the question of whether trapped surfaces are dynamical objects, meaning whether they can be formed in evolution starting with data devoid of trapped surfaces. This problem bears high physical significance and serves as a
test of reality
of black holes, in the following sense. The mathematical definition of a black hole region would be without physical meaning if it did not accurately capture what physicists perceive as black holes (this is more meaningful now than ever, as scientists recently succeeded in capturing the first-ever image of a black hole). Hence if "mathematical" black holes describe "physical" black holes, they should mathematically verify certain physical properties, one of which is dynamical formation.

The first results along this direction were obtained by Christodoulou for the Einstein equations coupled to a massless scalar field in spherical symmetry. Through a series of works
[
8
]
,
[
6
]
,
[
7
]
and
[
10
]
, Christodoulou managed to not only prove trapped surface formation, but to understand the picture of gravitational collapse in its entirety for the given model and under the given symmetry. The breakthrough in the absence of symmetry came in
[
9
]
by the same author. In this work, Christodoulou introduced a hierarchy of small and large components in the initial data which (almost) persists under the evolution of the Einstein equations. He termed his method the
short pulse
method. After Christodoulou, the work
[
12
]
by Klainerman-Rodnianski reduces the size of Christodoulou’s work from about 600 to approximately 120 pages, by using a slightly different hierarchy. Moreover, it reduces the number of derivatives of curvature required to prove semi-global existence from two to one. A few years later, An
[
3
]
introduces the signature for decay rates
s_{2}
on his way to proving an extension of
[
12
]
from a finite region to a region close to past null infinity. In 2014, An and Luk
[
5
]
prove the first
scale-critical
trapped surface formation criterion for the vacuum equations in the absence of symmetry. While Christodoulou’s data in
[
9
]
were large in
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, An and Luk give data which only have to be large in
H^{\frac{3}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, which is a scale-critical norm for the initial data. Taking advantage of the scale criticality in
[
5
]
, An
[
2
]
constructs initial data that give rise not merely to trapped surfaces, but an
apparent horizon
, a smooth 3-dimensional hypersurface consisting of marginally outer trapped surfaces. In 2019, An
[
1
]
produces a 55-page proof of trapped surface formation for the vacuum equations, making use of the signature for decay rates and obtaining an existence result from a region close to past null infinity. In
[
4
]
, An and the first author extend
[
1
]
to the case of the Einstein-Maxwell system.

1.2
Yang-Mills

The Non-abelian Yang-Mills dynamics has long been an area of high interest and intense investigation
[
17
,
18
,
19
,
20
,
21
]
. Classically, Yang-Mills fields correspond to special types of interacting waves and their rich non-linear structure due to the non-abelian characteristics at the level of partial differential equations has led to development of sophisticated techniques. Of course, at the quantized level Yang-Mills theory plays the most important role in the contemporary high energy particle physics. More specifically, non-abelian Yang-Mills fields describe Strong and weak interactions, two of the four fundamental forces present in nature. The pure Yang-Mills theory while quantized, however, exhibits a non-trivial mass gap
[
22
,
23
,
24
]
i.e., the excitation of the vacuum requires a non-zero energy and this fundamentally separates it from the U(1) Yang-Mills theory the electromagnetism where both classical and quantum spectra are gap-less. Classical Yang-Mills fields on the other hand, do not exhibit any spectral gap and therefore its existence is not expected. The scenario is substantially different while Yang-Mills fields are coupled to other sources. For example, the Yang-Mills fields that acquire mass at the classical level through interaction with scalar fields can exist in nature. For example, the carrier of the weak force
W
and
Z
bosons may exist classically due to the fact that they obtain mass through the spontaneous symmetry breaking (Higgs mechanism). In other words, while pure Yang-Mills fields can not exist in nature all by itself, coupling to other source field may lead to a different outcome. In this article, we want to investigate the dynamics of Yang-Mills field while coupled to gravity and possible condensation property.

Contrary to the Einstein-Maxwell system or Einstein-scalar field system, the Einstein-Yang-Mills equations are tremendously rich even in spherical symmetry and exhibit non-trivial dynamics. The numerical result of Bartnik
[
25
]
first showed the existence of a countable family of soliton-type solutions that are globally regular. Later Yau, Wasserman, and Smoller
[
26
]
rigorously proved the existence of such soliton-like solutions. However, such solutions were proven to be unstable against perturbations
[
27
]
.
[
28
]
first rigorously proved the existence of an infinite number of black hole solutions to the Einstein-Yang-Mills equations with gauge group SU(2) in spherical symmetry. The existence of these nontrivial solutions essentially unfolds the rich characteristics of the Einstein-Yang-Mills system. Due to the non-linear characteristics of the Yang-Mills fields, the fully coupled Einstein-Yang-Mills system is dynamically flexible i.e., both the possibility of the existence of regular solutions and the formation of singularities (naked or trapped behind a horizon) are open. This is precisely due to the fact that the non-linearity of Yang-Mills fields can counterbalance the non-linearity of gravity. The important feature, however, is that the nature of this balance is delicate and the possibility of collapse is open.

Motivated by these previous studies on spherically symmetric static Einstein-Yang-Mills system, one would naturally ask the following question "
given suitable initial data, can one prove the formation of an Einstein-Yang-Mills black hole in an evolutionary manner?
". Moreover, it is desirable to consider a generic data i.e., remove any symmetry assumption on the spacetime. The first step towards answering such a question would be prove the formation of a trapped surface. In a recent pre-print
[
13
]
, the second and third authors provided a semi-global existence result for the characteristic initial value problem for the Einstein-Yang-Mills equations for generic initial data. A natural next step would be to investigate if one can form trapped surface in an evolutionary manner. A naive though would be to incorporate a certain ‘largeness’ in the initial data. However, in the presence of gravity, this issue is far from obvious due to the possibility of a naked singularity. In addition, the gravity-Yang-Mills competition is delicate and one ought to identify the exact Yang-Mills curvature component (suitable gauge-invariant norm) on which to impose the largeness condition. The initial data should be chosen appropriately to be able to evolve the spacetime long enough to form a trapped surface. The most important issue that arises in the context of Yang-Mills theory is the choice of gauge for the Yang-Mills fields. Since Yang-Mills theory is a gauge theory after all, one ought to work in a particular choice of gauge (or equivalently descend to the ‘orbit space’ of the theory). Unfortunately, there does not exist a global gauge choice in Yang-Mills theory (in topological terms, one can not find a single chart to cover the entire orbit space
[
30
]
). The traditional choice of Lorentz gauge is known to develop finite time coordinate singularities in non-abelian theory contrary, to the linear Maxwell theory where such a breakdown is absent. In fact, the geometry of the orbit space of the theory (i.e., the space of connections modulo the bundle automorphisms) has a non-trivial effect in the matter of gauge choice
[
31
,
32
]
. The positivity of sectional curvature
[
31
]
of the orbit space leads to the development of the so-called ‘Gribov horizon’
[
29
]
which essentially indicates the breakdown of the so called ‘Coulomb’ gauge.

We circumvent the problem of Yang-Mills gauge choice in the current context by developing a novel gauge invariant framework for the Yang-Mills fields, where we write down the Yang-Mills equations in the double null gauge in terms of the fully gauge covariant derivative. By the virtue of the compatibility of the fiber metrics with the fully gauge covariant derivative, we are able to obtain the necessary gauge-invariant energy estimates
4
4

4

To our knowledge this gauge-invariant framework is not utilized in any previous work related to Yang-Mills equations.
. The true non-linear characteristics of the Yang-Mills theory manifest themselves in the higher-order energy estimates when we commute the fully gauge covariant derivatives. The estimates pass through to the orbit space (the true configuration space of the theory) by the virtue of its gauge-invariant property. The task, therefore, is to control these non-linear terms or, more precisely, preserve the smallness of appropriate weighted norms of them compared to the linear terms throughout the domain of existence. As it turns out, among the non-linearities present, the Yang-Mills non-liearities are the borderline ones, due to slow decay of Yang-Mills fields in the direction of past null infinity. In addition, we require control on certain additional null derivatives of the Yang-Mills curvature components. We discuss in due course how to handle this precisely. Once we obtain the necessary gauge invariant estimates, we can work in a particular collection of gauge choices to yield a semi-global existence result for the coupled theory. The latter part is standard and therefore we omit its discussion, focusing instead on the trapped surface formation mechanism.

1.3
Main Results

The main contribution of this paper is the following pair of theorems:

Theorem 1.2

(Semi-global existence result)
Given
\mathcal{I}>0
and a positive number
N\geq 3
, there exists an
a_{0}=a_{0}(\mathcal{I})
sufficiently large so that the following holds. Any smooth initial data set
(\hat{\chi},\alpha^{F})
to the Einstein-Yang-Mills system (
1.1
)-(
1.2
) satisfying the following conditions:

1)\hskip 14.22636pt\sup_{0\leq\underline{u}\leq 1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\leq N+7\\
m\leq 3\end{subarray}}a^{-\frac{1}{2}}\lVert\nabla_{4}^{m}(\lvert u_{\infty}\rvert\nabla)^{i}\hat{\chi}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{(S_{u_{\infty},\underline{u}})}}\leq\mathcal{I},

2)\hskip 14.22636pt\sup_{0\leq\underline{u}\leq 1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\leq N+7\\
m\leq 3\end{subarray}}a^{-\frac{1}{2}}\lVert\widehat{\nabla}_{4}^{m}(\lvert u_{\infty}\rvert\widehat{\nabla})^{i}\alpha^{F}\rVert_{L^{2}_{(S_{u_{\infty},\underline{u}})}}\leq\mathcal{I},

3)\hskip 14.22636pt\text{The initial data on}\hskip 5.69054pt\underline{u}=0\hskip 5.69054pt\text{are trivial (Minkowski)},

gives rise to a unique smooth solution to the Einstein-Yang-Mills system in the region

u_{\infty}\leq u\leq-\frac{a}{4},\hskip 5.69054pt0\leq\underline{u}\leq 1.

Theorem 1.3

(Formation of trapped surfaces)
With the notation of Theorem 1.1, if in addition to the assumptions of the previous Theorem, the initial data also satisfy the following isotropic condition:

4)\hskip 14.22636pt\int_{0}^{1}\lvert u_{\infty}\rvert^{2}\left(\lvert\hat{\chi}\rvert^{2}+\lvert\alpha^{F}\rvert_{\gamma,\delta}^{2}\right)(u_{\infty},\underline{u}^{\prime},\theta^{1},\theta^{2})\hskip 1.42262pt\text{d}\underline{u}^{\prime}\geq a,\hskip 5.69054pt\text{uniformly for all}\hskip 5.69054pt(\theta^{1},\theta^{2})\in\mathbb{S}^{2},

then the spacetime arising as a solution from Theorem 1.1 has a trapped surface at
S_{-\frac{a}{4},1}
.

Setup

2.1
Construction of the double null gauge

Denote by
L\mathcal{M}
the
frame bundle
of
\mathcal{M}
. We construct a double null gauge, meaning a smooth section of this bundle such that, through it, each point
p\in\mathcal{M}
maps to a renormalized frame
(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4})\in L\mathcal{M}
with
g(e_{3},e_{4})=-2
,
g(e_{A},e_{B})=\delta_{AB}
and
g(e_{3},e_{A})=g(e_{4},e_{A})=0
.

H_{u_{\infty}}(u=u_{\infty})
\underline{H}_{1}(\underline{u}=1)
\underline{H}_{0}(\underline{u}=0)
H_{u}
L^{\prime}
L^{\prime}
\underline{L}^{\prime}
\underline{L}^{\prime}

Figure 1:
Schematic depiction of the spacetime region of existence

We begin with two null hypersurfaces
H_{u_{\infty}},\underline{H}_{0}
and their intersection
S_{u_{\infty},0}
, a topological 2-sphere. For any point
q
on this
2-
sphere, the tangent space
T_{q}S_{u_{\infty},u}
is 2-dimensional and admits a
2-
dimensional orthogonal complement
T_{q}^{\perp}{S_{u_{\infty},0}}
, on which we can find two future-directed null vectors
L^{\prime}_{q}
and
\underline{L}^{\prime}_{q}
, normalized so that

g(L^{\prime}_{q},\underline{L}^{\prime}_{q})=-2.

The pair
\begin{Bmatrix}L^{\prime}_{q},\underline{L}^{\prime}_{q}\end{Bmatrix}
is uniquely determined up to a scaling factor
s>0

\begin{Bmatrix}L^{\prime}_{q},\underline{L}^{\prime}_{q}\end{Bmatrix}\mapsto\begin{Bmatrix}sL^{\prime}_{q},s^{-1}\underline{L}^{\prime}_{q}\end{Bmatrix}.

Starting from
q
and initially tangent to
L^{\prime}_{q}
, a unique geodesic is sent out. Call this geodesic,
l_{q}
. We extend the vectorfield
L^{\prime}
along
l_{q}
by parallel transport:
D_{L^{\prime}}L^{\prime}=0
. It then follows by simple calculation that
l_{q}
is null, so that
g(L^{\prime},L^{\prime})=0
along
l_{q}
. Gathering the
\begin{Bmatrix}l_{q}\end{Bmatrix}
together we get a null hypersurface
H_{u_{\infty}}
. The null hypersurface
\underline{H}_{0}
is obtained similarly.
Note that, by construction, given a point
p
on
H_{u_{\infty}}
or
\underline{H}_{0}
, in the corresponding tangent spaces, there is a preferred null vector
L^{\prime}_{p}
or
\underline{L}^{\prime}_{p}
.

We next choose a lapse function
\Omega
, which we define to be equal to 1 on
S_{u_{\infty},0}
and then extend as a continuous function along both initial null hypersurfaces
5
5

5

Indeed, there is a gauge freedom in choosing
\Omega
on the initial hypersurfaces.
. Define the vector fields

L:=\Omega^{2}L^{\prime}\hskip 5.69054pt\text{along}\hskip 5.69054ptH_{u_{\infty}}\hskip 5.69054pt\text{and}\hskip 5.69054pt\underline{L}:=\Omega^{2}\underline{L}^{\prime}\hskip 5.69054pt\text{along}\hskip 5.69054pt\underline{H}_{0}.

We use these vector fields to define two functions

\underline{u}\hskip 5.69054pt\text{on}\hskip 5.69054ptH_{u_{\infty}}\hskip 5.69054pt\text{satisfying}\hskip 5.69054ptL\underline{u}=1\hskip 5.69054pt\text{on}\hskip 5.69054ptH_{u_{\infty}}\hskip 5.69054pt\text{and}\hskip 5.69054pt\underline{u}=0\hskip 5.69054pt\text{on}\hskip 5.69054ptS_{u_{\infty},0},

u\hskip 5.69054pt\text{on}\hskip 5.69054pt\underline{H}_{0}\hskip 5.69054pt\text{satisfying}\hskip 5.69054pt\underline{L}u=1\hskip 5.69054pt\text{on}\hskip 5.69054pt\underline{H}_{0}\hskip 5.69054pt\text{and}\hskip 5.69054ptu=0\hskip 5.69054pt\text{on}\hskip 5.69054ptS_{u_{\infty},0}.

We now use these (so-called
optical
) functions to proceed further with the construction. Let
S_{u_{\infty},\underline{u}^{\prime}}
be the embedded
2-
surface on
H_{u_{\infty}}
on which
\underline{u}=\underline{u}^{\prime}
and define
S_{u,0}
similarly. At each point
p\in S_{u_{\infty},\underline{u}^{\prime}}
we have constructed a preferred null vector
L^{\prime}_{p}
. It follows that we can uniquely determine an incoming
g-
null vector
\underline{L}^{\prime}_{p}
satisfying
g(L^{\prime}_{p},\underline{L}^{\prime}_{p})=-2
. Let
\underline{l}_{p}
be the unique geodesic emanating from
p
with tangent vector
\underline{L}^{\prime}_{p}
. We extend the definition of
\underline{L}^{\prime}
along
\underline{l}_{q}
by parallel transport, so that
D_{\underline{L}^{\prime}}\underline{L}^{\prime}=0
. Gathering all the
\begin{Bmatrix}\underline{l}_{p}\end{Bmatrix}
on
S_{u_{\infty},\underline{u}^{\prime}}
, we thus obtain the null hypersurface
\underline{H}_{\underline{u}^{\prime}}
. We obtain the null hypersurface
H_{u^{\prime}}
in an analogous way and define
S_{u,\underline{u}}:=H_{u}\cap\underline{H}_{\underline{u}}
. Having constructed the vector fields
L^{\prime}
and
\underline{L}^{\prime}
in all of the spacetime region, we extend the definition of the lapse function
\Omega
by requiring, at each point
p\in S_{u,\underline{u}}
that

g(L^{\prime}_{p},\underline{L}^{\prime}_{p})=-2{\left.\kern-1.2pt\Omega^{-2}\right|_{p}}.

The incoming null hypersurfaces
\begin{Bmatrix}\underline{H}_{\underline{u}}\end{Bmatrix}_{0\leq\underline{u}\leq 1}
and outgoing null hypersurfaces
\begin{Bmatrix}H_{u}\end{Bmatrix}_{-\frac{a}{4}\leq u\leq u_{\infty}}
along with their pairwise intersections
S_{u,\underline{u}}
together define a
double null foliation
on the spacetime. On a given
S_{u,\underline{u}}
, we have
g(\Omega L^{\prime},\Omega\underline{L}^{\prime})=-2
and hence the vectors

e_{3}:=\Omega\underline{L}^{\prime},e_{4}:=\Omega L^{\prime}

define a normalized null pair at each point on the sphere. We make the gauge choice
\Omega\equiv 1
along both initial hypersurfaces.

2.2
Choice of coordinates and expression of the metric

To define angular coordinates on each
S_{u,\underline{u}}
in a smooth way, we begin by defining angular coordinates on
S_{u_{\infty},0}
. Since this is a standard 2-sphere in Minkowki space, we can use the stereographic projection coordinates
(\theta^{1},\hskip 1.42262pt\theta^{2})
on
S_{u_{\infty},0}
. We first extend this coordinate to the whole of
\underline{H}_{0}
by insisting that
\not{\mathcal{L}}_{\underline{L}}\theta^{A}=0
on
\underline{H}_{0}
for
A=1,\hskip 1.42262pt2
and then to the whole spacetime by insisting that, for all
u
,
\not{\mathcal{L}}_{L}\theta^{A}=0
, where
L
initially starts normal to some
S_{u,0}
. As such we have established a coordinate system
(u,\underline{u},\theta^{1},\theta^{2})
in a neighbourhood of the initial sphere. In these coordinates, the vectors
e_{3},\hskip 1.42262pte_{4}
become

e_{3}=\Omega^{-1}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial u}+b^{A}\hskip 1.42262pt\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta^{A}}\right),\hskip 5.69054pte_{4}=\Omega^{-1}\frac{\partial}{\partial\underline{u}}

and the metric now takes the following form:

g=-2\Omega^{2}\left(\text{d}u\otimes\text{d}\underline{u}+\text{d}\underline{u}\otimes\text{d}u\right)+\not{g}_{AB}\left(\text{d}\theta^{A}-b^{A}\text{d}u\right)\otimes\left(\text{d}\theta^{B}-b^{B}\text{d}u\right)

(2.1)

It is a requirement that
b^{A}\equiv 0
on
\underline{H}_{0}
. The section that maps
p\in\mathcal{M}\mapsto\left({\left.\kern-1.2pt\theta^{1}\right|_{p}},{\left.\kern-1.2pt\theta^{2}\right|_{p}}{\left.\kern-1.2pte_{3}\right|_{p}},{\left.\kern-1.2pte_{4}\right|_{p}}\right)
is the double null gauge we wanted to construct.

2.3
Yang-Mills Theory

Now we define a Yang-Mills theory on
(\mathcal{M},g)
. We denote by
\mathfrak{P}
a
C^{\infty}
principal bundle with base a
3+1
dimensional Lorentzian manifold
M
and a Lie group
G
. We assume that
G
is compact semi-simple (for physical purposes) and therefore admits a positive definite non-degenerate bi-invariant metric. Its Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
by construction admits an adjoint invariant, positive definite scalar product denoted by
\langle~,~\rangle
which enjoys the property: for
A,B,C\in\mathfrak{g}
,

\displaystyle\langle[A,B],C\rangle=\langle A,[B,C]\rangle.

(2.2)

as a consequence of adjoint invariance.
A Yang-Mills connection is defined as a
1-
form
w
on
\mathfrak{P}
with values in
\mathfrak{g}
endowed with compatibility properties. Its representative in a local trivialization of
\mathfrak{P}
over
U\subset\mathcal{M}
,

\displaystyle\varphi:p\mapsto(x,a),~p\in\mathfrak{P},~x\in U,~a\in G

(2.3)

is the
1-
form
s^{*}w
on
U
, where
s
is the local section of
\mathfrak{P}
corresponding canonically to the local trivialization
s(x)=\varphi^{-1}(x,e)
, called a
gauge
. Let
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
be representatives of
w
in gauges
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
over
U_{1}\subset\mathcal{M}
and
U_{2}\in\mathcal{M}
. In
U_{1}\cap U_{2}
, one has

\displaystyle A_{1}=Ad(u^{-1}_{12})A_{2}+u_{12}\Theta_{MC},

(2.4)

where
\Theta_{MC}
is the Maurer-Cartan form on
G
, and
u_{12}:U_{1}\cap U_{2}\to G
generates the transformation between the two local trivializations:

\displaystyle s_{1}=R_{u_{12}}s_{2},

(2.5)

R_{u_{12}}
is the right translation on
\mathfrak{P}
by
u_{12}
. Given the principal bundle
\mathfrak{P}\to\mathcal{M}
, a Yang-Mills potential
A
on
\mathcal{M}
is a section of the fibered tensor product
T^{*}M\otimes_{M}\mathfrak{P}_{Affine,\mathfrak{g}}
where
\mathfrak{P}_{Affine,\mathfrak{g}}
is the affine bundle with base
M
and typical fibre
\mathfrak{g}
, associated to
\mathfrak{P}
via relation (
2.4
). If
\widehat{A}
is another Yang-Mills potential on
M
, then
A-\widehat{A}
is a section of the tensor product of vector bundles
T^{*}M\otimes_{M}P_{Ad,\mathfrak{g}}
, where
\mathfrak{P}_{Ad,\mathfrak{g}}:=\mathfrak{P}\times_{Ad}\mathfrak{g}
is the vector bundle associated to
\mathfrak{P}
by the adjoint representation of
G
on
\mathfrak{g}
. There is a inner product in the fibres of
\mathfrak{P}_{Ad,\mathfrak{g}}
, induced from that on
\mathfrak{g}
. The curvature
\mathbf{C}
of the connection
w
considered as a
1-
form on
\mathfrak{P}
is a
\mathfrak{g}
-valued
2-
form on
\mathfrak{P}
. Its representative in a gauge where
w
is represented by
A
is given by

\displaystyle F:=dA+[A,A],

(2.6)

and relation between two representatives
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
on
U_{1}\cap U_{2}
is
F_{1}=Ad(u^{-1}_{12})F_{2}
and therefore
F
is a section of the vector bundle
\Lambda^{2}T^{*}M\otimes_{M}\mathfrak{P}_{Ad,\mathfrak{g}}
. For a section
\mathfrak{O}
of the vector bundle
\otimes^{k}T^{*}M\otimes_{M}\mathfrak{P}_{Ad,\mathfrak{g}}
, a natural covariant derivative is defined as follows

\displaystyle\widehat{D}\mathfrak{O}:=D\mathfrak{O}+[A,\mathfrak{O}],

(2.7)

where
D
is the usual covariant derivative induced by the Lorentzian structure of
M
and by construction
\widehat{D}\mathfrak{O}
is a section of the vector bundle
\otimes^{k+1}T^{*}M\otimes_{M}\mathfrak{P}_{Ad,\mathfrak{g}}
. The Yang-Mills coupling constant
g_{YM}
is set to 1. If the space of connections in a particular Sobolev class is denoted by
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{G}
is the automorphism group of the bundle
\mathfrak{P}
, then the true configuration space (or the orbit space) of the theory is
\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{G}
6
6

6

\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{G}
is an infinite dimensional manifold modulo certain additional criteria
.

The classical Yang-Mills equations (in the absence of sources)
correspond to setting the natural (spacetime and gauge as defined in
2.7
) covariant divergence of this curvature
two-form
F
to zero. By virtue of its definition in terms of the connection, this curvature also satisfies
the Bianchi identity that asserts the vanishing of its gauge covariant exterior derivative. Taken
together, these equations provide a geometrically natural nonlinear generalization of Maxwell’s
equations (when the latter are written in terms of a ‘vector potential’) and of course play a
fundamental role in modern elementary particle physics. If nontrivial bundles are considered
or nontrivial spacetime topologies are involved, then the foregoing so-called ‘local trivializations’ of the bundles in question must be patched together to give global descriptions but, by virtue of the covariance of the formalism, there is a natural way of carrying out this patching procedure, at least over those regions of spacetime where the connections are well-defined.

We choose a vector space
V
and a matrix representation for the action of
G
on
V
. For simplicity, let us confine our attention to real representations though in fact this restriction is inessential. We now consider vector bundles over spacetime (so-called ‘associated’ bundles) with standard fiber
\simeq V
. Cross sections of such bundles would represent, in physical terms, multiplets of Higgs fields. To formulate field equations for such Higgs fields that are naturally covariant with respect to automorphisms of the associated vector bundle (i.e., with respect to gauge transformations acting on the Higgs fields) one needs a covariant derivative operator
\widehat{D}
or connection defined on this bundle. Such an object is naturally induced from a ‘fundamental’ connection on the principal
G
-bundle described above and in turn induces (when expressed relative to a local trivialization) a one-form on (some local chart for) the base manifold with values in the chosen matrix representation for the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. Let us consider the dimension of the group
G
to be
dim_{G}
and since
\mathfrak{g}:=T_{e}G
, it has a natural vector space structure. Assume that the vector space
\mathfrak{g}
has a basis
\{\chi_{A}\}_{A=1}^{dim_{G}}
given by a set of
k\times k
real valued matrices (
k
being the dimension of the representation
V
of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
). The connection
1-
form field is then defined to be

\displaystyle A:=A^{A}_{\mu}\chi_{A}dx^{\mu}=A^{A}_{\mu}(\chi_{A})^{P}_{Q}dx^{\mu}=A^{P}~_{Q\mu}dx^{\mu},~P,Q=1,2,3,...,k.

(2.8)

From now on by the connection 1-form field
A_{\mu}
, we will always mean
A^{P}~_{Q\mu}
. In the current setting
A\in\Omega^{1}(M;End(V))
, where
End(V)
denotes the space of endomorphisms of the vector space
V
. The curvature of this connection is defined to be the Yang-Mills field
F\in\Omega^{2}(M;End(V))

\displaystyle F^{P}~_{Q\mu\nu}:=\partial_{\mu}A^{P}~_{Q\nu}-\partial_{\nu}A^{P}~_{Q\mu}+[A,A]^{P}~_{Q\mu\nu},

(2.9)

where the bracket is defined on the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
and given by the commutator of matrices under multiplication. The Yang-Mills coupling constant is set to unity. Since
G
is compact, it admits a positive definite adjoint invariant metric on
\mathfrak{g}
. We choose a basis of
\mathfrak{g}
such that this adjoint invariant metric takes the Cartesian form
\delta_{AB}
and work with representations for which the bases satisfy

\displaystyle-tr(\chi_{A}\chi_{B})=(\chi_{A})^{P}_{Q}(\chi_{A})^{Q}_{P}=\delta_{AB}.

(2.10)

Under a gauge transformation by
\mathcal{U}
, the
\mathfrak{g}
-valued 1-form field
A
transforms as

\displaystyle A_{\mu}\mapsto\mathcal{U}^{-1}A_{\mu}\mathcal{U}+\mathcal{U}\partial_{\mu}\mathcal{U}^{-1}

(2.11)

and therefore
A_{\mu}
is not a tensor in the sense that it is
not
a
(1,1)
section of the associated
V-
bundle over
\mathcal{D}_{u,\bar{u}}
. For any
\mathfrak{g}
-valued section
\mathcal{K}^{P}~_{Q\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}....\mu_{k}}
of a vector bundle over
\mathcal{D}_{u,\bar{u}}
that transforms as a tensor under the gauge transformation, the gauge covariant derivative is defined to be

\displaystyle\hat{D}_{\alpha}\mathcal{K}^{P}~_{Q\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}....\mu_{k}}:=D_{\alpha}\mathcal{K}^{P}~_{Q\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}....\mu_{k}}+A^{P}~_{R\alpha}\mathcal{K}^{R}~_{Q\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}....\mu_{k}}-A^{R}~_{Q\alpha}\mathcal{K}^{P}~_{R\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}....\mu_{k}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D_{\alpha}\mathcal{K}^{P}~_{Q\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}....\mu_{k}}+[A,\mathcal{K}]^{P}~_{Q\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}....\mu_{k}},

(2.12)

where
D_{\alpha}
is the ordinary spacetime covariant derivative with respect to a Lorentzian metric on
\mathcal{D}_{u,\bar{u}}
. Even though the connection of the gauge bundle appears in the definition of the gauge covariant derivative, we will never make explicit use of it in the current context, but rather work with the fully gauge covariant derivative
\hat{D}
. More specifically, in our analysis, we will encounter the commutator of the fully gauge covariant derivative which yields Riemann curvature and Yang-Mills curvature components. In other words, using the fully gauge covariant derivative, we do not encounter any connection terms, allowing us to obtain estimates in a gauge-invariant way. The commutator of the fully gauge covariant derivative while acting on a
\mathfrak{g}
-valued section of a vector bundle
\mathcal{K}^{P}~_{Q\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}....\mu_{k}}
(or a section of the mixed bundle) yields

\displaystyle[\hat{D}_{\alpha},\hat{D}_{\beta}]\mathcal{K}^{P}~_{Q\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}....\mu_{k}}=

\displaystyle F^{P}~_{R\alpha\beta}\mathcal{K}^{R}~_{Q\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}....\mu_{k}}-F^{R}~_{Q\alpha\beta}\mathcal{K}^{P}~_{R\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}....\mu_{k}}

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\sum_{i}R^{\gamma}~_{\mu_{i}\alpha\beta}\mathcal{K}^{P}~_{Q\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}...\hat{\gamma}....\mu_{k}},

(2.13)

where
\hat{\gamma}
indicates the removal of the index
\hat{\mu}_{i}
and replacing by
\gamma
. Note that
\hat{D}
is compatible with both the metrics and therefore the commutator produces curvature of the mixed bundle (Yang-Mills curvature and spacetime curvature). Action of
\hat{D}
is only well-defined on
sections
of the mixed bundle.

2.4
The E-Y-M system expressed in the double null gauge

In this section we are going to express the Einstein-Yang-Mills system in the double null gauge given above. We begin by decomposing curvature components and Ricci coefficients with respect to the frame
(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4})
.
Let
A,B
take values in
\begin{Bmatrix}1,2\end{Bmatrix}
. We define the following (Weyl) null curvature components:

\alpha_{AB}:=W(e_{A},e_{4},e_{B},e_{4}),\hskip 5.69054pt\underline{\alpha}_{AB}:=W(e_{A},e_{3},e_{B},e_{3}),

\beta_{A}:=\frac{1}{2}W(e_{A},e_{4},e_{3},e_{4}),\hskip 5.69054pt\underline{\beta}_{A}:=\frac{1}{2}W(e_{A},e_{3},e_{3},e_{4}),

\rho:=\frac{1}{4}W(e_{3},e_{4},e_{3},e_{4}),\hskip 5.69054pt\sigma=\frac{1}{4}\prescript{*}{}{W}(e_{3},e_{4},e_{3},e_{4}).

Moreover, for reasons to be explained later on, we define the
renormalized curvature components

\beta^{R}_{A}=\beta_{A}-\frac{1}{2}R_{A4},\hskip 5.69054pt\underline{\beta}^{R}_{A}=\underline{\beta}_{A}+\frac{1}{2}R_{A3}.

(2.14)

For the Ricci coefficients, we decompose as follows:

\chi_{AB}:=g(D_{A}e_{4},\hskip 1.42262pte_{B}),\hskip 5.69054pt\underline{\chi}_{AB}:=g(D_{A}e_{3},e_{B}),

\eta_{A}:=-\frac{1}{2}g(D_{A}e_{3},\hskip 1.42262pte_{4}),\hskip 5.69054pt\underline{\eta}_{A}:=-\frac{1}{2}g(D_{A}e_{4},e_{3}),

\omega:=-\frac{1}{4}g(D_{4}e_{3},\hskip 1.42262pte_{4}),\hskip 5.69054pt\underline{\omega}:=-\frac{1}{4}g(D_{3}e_{4},\hskip 1.42262pte_{3}),

\zeta_{A}:=\frac{1}{2}g(D_{A}e_{4},\hskip 1.42262pte_{3}).

Moreover, if
\gamma
denotes the induced metric on
S_{u,\underline{u}}
, we make the following further decomposition:

\chi=\hat{\chi}+\frac{1}{2}\mbox{tr}\chi\gamma,\hskip 5.69054pt\underline{\chi}=\underline{\hat{\chi}}+\frac{1}{2}\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}\hskip 1.42262pt\gamma.

The Yang-Mills curvature 2-form
F
is also decomposed into null components as follows:

\displaystyle\alpha_{A}^{F}:={{F}^{P}}_{Q}(e_{A},e_{4}),\hskip 5.69054pt\underline{\alpha}_{A}^{F}:={{F}^{P}}_{Q}(e_{A},e_{3}),

(2.15)

\displaystyle\rho^{F}:=\frac{1}{2}{{F}^{P}}_{Q}(e_{3},e_{4}),\hskip 5.69054pt\sigma^{F}:=\frac{1}{2}\prescript{*}{}{{{F}^{P}}_{Q}}(e_{3},e_{4})={{F}^{P}}_{Q}(e_{1},e_{2}).

(2.16)

For the Yang-Mills energy-momentum tensor
\mathfrak{T}_{\mu\nu}
, there holds
g^{\alpha\beta}\mathfrak{T}_{\alpha\beta}=0
, whence it follows that the scalar curvature vanishes and the Einstein equations reduce to
R_{\mu\nu}=\mathfrak{T}_{\mu\nu}
.

Before we are ready to present the equations, we introduce a few basic definitions. First of all, denote by
\nabla
and
\widehat{\nabla}
the covariant derivative operators induced by
D
and
\hat{D}
on
S_{u,\underline{u}}
respectively. Let
\nabla_{3},\nabla_{4}
denote the projections of the covariant derivatives
D_{3}
and
D_{4}
to
S_{u,\underline{u}}
and define, in a similar fashion,
\widehat{\nabla}_{3}
and
\widehat{\nabla}_{4}
using
\hat{D}
. For two
1-
forms
\phi_{A}^{1},\hskip 1.42262pt\phi_{A}^{2}
, we define

(\phi_{1}\hat{\otimes}\phi_{2})_{AB}:=\phi_{A}^{1}\hskip 1.42262pt\phi_{B}^{2}+\phi_{B}^{1}\hskip 1.42262pt\phi_{A}^{2}-\gamma_{AB}\hskip 1.42262pt(\phi^{1}\cdot\phi^{2}),

while for symmetric
2-
tensors
\phi_{AB}^{1},\hskip 1.42262pt\phi_{AB}^{2}
, we define

(\phi^{1}\wedge\phi^{2})_{AB}:=\not{\epsilon}^{AB}\hskip 1.42262pt(\gamma^{-1})^{CD}\hskip 1.42262pt\phi_{AB}^{1}\hskip 1.42262pt\phi_{CD}^{2}.

Here
\not{\epsilon}
is the volume form associated with the metric
\gamma
. Moreover, by
\phi^{1}\cdot\phi^{2}
we mean an arbitrary contraction of the tensor product of
\phi^{1}
and
\phi^{2}
with respect to the metric
\gamma
. We also define suitable trace, divergence and curl operators. For totally symmetric tensors
\phi
, we define these operators as follows:

(\mbox{div }\phi)_{A_{1}\dots A_{r}}:=\nabla^{B}\phi_{BA_{1}\dots A_{r}},

(\mbox{curl }\phi)_{A_{1}\dots A_{r}}:=\not{\epsilon}^{BC}\nabla_{B}\phi_{CA_{1}\dots A_{r}},

(\mbox{tr}\phi)_{A_{1}\dots A_{r-1}}:=(\gamma^{-1})^{BC}\phi_{BCA_{1}\dots A_{r-1}}.

Be it noted that the operators
\widehat{\mbox{div }}
and
\widehat{\mbox{curl }}
can be defined similarly on sections of the mixed bundle. Furthermore, we introduce the
*
operator on
1-
forms and
2-
tensors:

\prescript{*}{}{\phi}_{A}:=\gamma_{AC}\not{\epsilon}^{CB}\hskip 1.42262pt\phi_{B},

\prescript{*}{}{\phi}_{AB}:=\gamma_{BD}\hskip 1.42262pt\not{\epsilon}^{DC}\hskip 1.42262pt\phi_{AC}.

Finally, on a
1-
form
\phi
, the operator
\nabla\hat{\otimes}
is defined as follows:

(\nabla\hat{\otimes}\phi)_{A}:=\nabla_{B}\phi_{A}+\nabla_{A}\phi_{B}-\gamma_{AB}\hskip 1.42262pt\mbox{div }\phi.

Expressed in the double null gauge, the Einstein-Yang-Mills system turns into a system of null structure, null Bianchi and null Yang-Mills equations, which take the following form:

\displaystyle\nabla_{4}\mbox{tr}\chi+\frac{1}{2}(\mbox{tr}\chi)^{2}=-|\hat{\chi}|^{2}_{\gamma}-2\omega\mbox{tr}\chi-\mathfrak{T}_{44}

(2.17)

\displaystyle\nabla_{4}\hat{\chi}+\mbox{tr}\chi\hat{\chi}=-2\omega\hat{\chi}-\alpha

(2.18)

\displaystyle\nabla_{3}tr\underline{\chi}+\frac{1}{2}(tr\underline{\chi})^{2}=-|\hat{\underline{\chi}}|^{2}_{\gamma}-2\underline{\omega}tr\underline{\chi}-\mathfrak{T}_{33}

(2.19)

\displaystyle\nabla_{3}\hat{\underline{\chi}}+tr\underline{\chi}\hat{\underline{\chi}}=-2\underline{\omega}\hat{\underline{\chi}}-\underline{\alpha}

(2.20)

\displaystyle\nabla_{4}\eta_{a}=-\chi\cdot(\eta-\underline{\eta})-\beta-\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{T}_{a4}

(2.21)

\displaystyle\nabla_{3}\underline{\eta}_{a}=-\underline{\chi}\cdot(\underline{\eta}-\eta)+\underline{\beta}+\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{T}_{a3}

(2.22)

\displaystyle\nabla_{4}\underline{\omega}=2\omega\underline{\omega}+\frac{3}{4}|\eta-\underline{\eta}|^{2}-\frac{1}{4}(\eta-\underline{\eta})\cdot(\eta+\underline{\eta})-\frac{1}{8}|\eta+\underline{\eta}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\rho+\frac{1}{4}\mathfrak{T}_{43}

(2.23)

\displaystyle\nabla_{3}\omega=2\omega\underline{\omega}+\frac{3}{4}|\eta-\underline{\eta}|^{2}+\frac{1}{4}(\eta-\underline{\eta})\cdot(\eta+\underline{\eta})-\frac{1}{8}|\eta+\underline{\eta}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\rho+\frac{1}{4}\mathfrak{T}_{43}

(2.24)

\displaystyle\nabla_{4}tr\underline{\chi}+\frac{1}{2}\mbox{tr}\chi tr\underline{\chi}=2\omega tr\underline{\chi}+2\text{div}\underline{\eta}+2|\underline{\eta}|^{2}_{\gamma}+2\rho-\hat{\chi}\cdot\hat{\underline{\chi}}

(2.25)

\displaystyle\nabla_{3}\mbox{tr}\chi+\frac{1}{2}tr\underline{\chi}\mbox{tr}\chi=2\underline{\omega}\mbox{tr}\chi+2\text{div}\eta+2|\eta|^{2}+2\rho-\hat{\chi}\cdot\hat{\underline{\chi}}

(2.26)

\displaystyle\nabla_{4}\hat{\underline{\chi}}+\frac{1}{2}\mbox{tr}\chi\hat{\underline{\chi}}=\nabla\hat{\otimes}\underline{\eta}+2\omega\hat{\underline{\chi}}-\frac{1}{2}tr\underline{\chi}\hat{\chi}+\underline{\eta}\hat{\otimes}\underline{\eta}+\hat{\mathfrak{T}}_{ab}

(2.27)

\displaystyle\nabla_{3}\hat{\chi}+\frac{1}{2}tr\underline{\chi}\hat{\chi}=\nabla\hat{\otimes}\eta+2\underline{\omega}\hat{\chi}-\frac{1}{2}\mbox{tr}\chi\hat{\underline{\chi}}+\eta\hat{\otimes}\eta+\hat{\mathfrak{T}}_{ab}

(2.28)

\displaystyle\text{div}\hat{\chi}=\frac{1}{2}\nabla\mbox{tr}\chi-\frac{1}{2}(\eta-\underline{\eta})\cdot(\hat{\chi}-\frac{1}{2}\mbox{tr}\chi\gamma)-\beta+\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{T}(e_{4},\cdot)

(2.29)

\displaystyle\text{div}\hat{\underline{\chi}}=\frac{1}{2}\nabla tr\underline{\chi}-\frac{1}{2}(\underline{\eta}-\eta)\cdot(\hat{\underline{\chi}}-\frac{1}{2}tr\underline{\chi}\gamma)-\underline{\beta}+\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{T}(e_{3},\cdot)

(2.30)

\displaystyle\text{curl}\eta=\hat{\underline{\chi}}\wedge\hat{\chi}+\sigma\epsilon=-\text{curl}\underline{\eta}

(2.31)

\displaystyle K-\frac{1}{2}\hat{\chi}\cdot\hat{\underline{\chi}}+\frac{1}{4}\mbox{tr}\chi tr\underline{\chi}=-\rho+\frac{1}{4}\mathfrak{T}_{43},

(2.32)

\displaystyle\nabla_{4}\zeta=2\nabla\omega+\chi\cdot(\underline{\eta}-\zeta)+2\omega(\zeta+\underline{\eta})-\beta-\sigma^{F}\epsilon\cdot\alpha^{F}-\rho^{F}\cdot\alpha^{F},

(2.33)

\displaystyle\nabla_{3}\zeta=-2\nabla\omega-\underline{\chi}\cdot(\zeta+\eta)+2\underline{\omega}(\zeta-\eta)-\underline{\beta}+\epsilon\sigma^{F}\cdot\underline{\alpha}^{F}-\rho^{F}\cdot\underline{\alpha}^{F}.

(2.34)

The Bianchi equations then read as follows:

\begin{split}\nabla_{3}\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}\alpha=&\nabla\hat{\otimes}\beta+4\underline{\omega}\alpha-3\left(\hat{\chi}\rho+\prescript{*}{}{\hat{\chi}}\sigma\right)+(\zeta+4\eta)\hat{\otimes}\beta\\
&-D_{4}R_{AB}+\frac{1}{2}\left(D_{B}R_{4A}+D_{A}R_{4B}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left(D_{4}R_{43}-D_{3}R_{44}\right)\not{g}_{AB},\end{split}

(2.35)

\nabla_{4}\beta+2\mbox{tr}\chi\beta=\text{div}\alpha-2\omega\beta+\left(\eta-2\zeta\right)\cdot\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\left(D_{4}R_{4A}-D_{A}R_{44}\right),

(2.36)

\nabla_{3}\beta+\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}\beta=\nabla\rho+\prescript{*}{}{\nabla}\sigma+2\underline{\omega}\beta+2\hat{\chi}\cdot\underline{\beta}+3\left(\eta\rho+\prescript{*}{}{\eta}\sigma\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left(D_{A}R_{43}-D_{4}R_{3A}\right),

(2.37)

\nabla_{4}\sigma+\frac{3}{2}\mbox{tr}\chi\sigma=-\text{div}\prescript{*}{}{\beta}+\frac{1}{2}\hskip 1.42262pt\underline{\hat{\chi}}\cdot\prescript{*}{}{\alpha}-(\zeta+2\underline{\eta})\cdot\prescript{*}{}{\beta}-\frac{1}{4}\left(D_{\mu}R_{4\nu}-D_{\nu}R_{4\mu}\right){\epsilon^{\mu\nu}}_{34},

(2.38)

\nabla_{3}\sigma+\frac{3}{2}\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}\sigma=-\text{div}\prescript{*}{}{\underline{\beta}}+\frac{1}{2}\hskip 1.42262pt\hat{\chi}\cdot\prescript{*}{}{\underline{\alpha}}-(\zeta+2\eta)\cdot\prescript{*}{}{\underline{\beta}}+\frac{1}{4}\left(D_{\mu}R_{3\nu}-0D_{\nu}R_{3\mu}\right){\epsilon^{\mu\nu}}_{34},

(2.39)

\nabla_{4}\rho+\frac{3}{2}\mbox{tr}\chi\rho=\text{\mbox{div }}\beta-\frac{1}{2}\underline{\hat{\chi}}\cdot\alpha+(\zeta+2\underline{\eta})\cdot\beta-\frac{1}{4}\left(D_{3}R_{44}-D_{4}R_{43}\right),

(2.40)

\nabla_{3}\rho+\frac{3}{2}\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}\rho=-\text{div}\underline{\beta}-\frac{1}{2}\hat{\chi}\cdot\underline{\alpha}+(\zeta-2\eta)\cdot\underline{\beta}+\frac{1}{4}\left(D_{3}R_{34}-D_{4}R_{33}\right),

(2.41)

\nabla_{4}\underline{\beta}+\mbox{tr}\chi\underline{\beta}=-\nabla\rho+\prescript{*}{}{\nabla}\sigma+2\omega\underline{\beta}+2\underline{\hat{\chi}}\cdot\beta-3\left(\underline{\eta}\rho-\prescript{*}{}{\underline{\eta}}\sigma\right)-\frac{1}{2}\left(D_{A}R_{43}-D_{3}R_{4A}\right),

(2.42)

\nabla_{3}\underline{\beta}+2\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}\underline{\beta}=-\text{div}\underline{\alpha}-2\underline{\omega}\underline{\beta}+\underline{\eta}\cdot\underline{\alpha}+\frac{1}{2}\left(D_{A}R_{33}-D_{3}R_{3A}\right),

(2.43)

\begin{split}\nabla_{4}\underline{\alpha}+\frac{1}{2}\mbox{tr}\chi\hskip 1.42262pt\underline{\alpha}=&-\nabla\hat{\otimes}\underline{\beta}+4\omega\hskip 1.42262pt\underline{\alpha}-3\left(\underline{\hat{\chi}}\rho-\prescript{*}{}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}}\sigma\right)+\left(\zeta-4\underline{\eta}\right)\hat{\otimes}\underline{\beta}\\
&-D_{3}R_{AB}+\frac{1}{2}\left(D_{A}R_{3B}+D_{B}R_{3A}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left(D_{3}R_{34}-D_{4}R_{43}\right)\not{g}_{AB}.\end{split}

(2.44)

Moreover, the Yang-Mills equations,

\widehat{D}_{\mu}{{F^{P}}}_{Q\nu\lambda}+\widehat{D}_{\nu}{{F^{P}}}_{Q\lambda\mu}+\widehat{D}_{\lambda}{{F}^{P}}_{Q\mu\nu}=0,\hskip 5.69054ptg^{\alpha\beta}\widehat{D}_{\alpha}{F^{P}}_{Q\beta\mu}=0,

(2.45)

take the following form:

\widehat{\nabla}_{4}\underline{\alpha}^{F}+\frac{1}{2}\mbox{tr}\chi\underline{\alpha}^{F}=-\widehat{\nabla}\rho^{F}+\prescript{*}{}{\widehat{\nabla}}\sigma^{F}-2\prescript{*}{}{\underline{\eta}}\sigma^{F}-2\underline{\eta}\rho^{F}+2\omega\underline{\alpha}^{F}-\underline{\hat{\chi}}\cdot\alpha^{F},

(2.46)

\widehat{\nabla}_{3}\alpha^{F}+\frac{1}{2}\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}\alpha^{F}=\widehat{\nabla}\rho^{F}+\prescript{*}{}{\widehat{\nabla}}\sigma^{F}-2\prescript{*}{}{\eta}\sigma^{F}+2\eta\rho^{F}+2\underline{\omega}\alpha^{F}-\hat{\chi}\cdot\underline{\alpha}^{F},

(2.47)

\widehat{\nabla}_{4}\rho^{F}=\widehat{\text{div}}\alpha^{F}-\mbox{tr}\chi\rho^{F}-\left(\eta-\underline{\eta}\right)\cdot\alpha^{F},

(2.48)

\widehat{\nabla}_{4}\sigma^{F}=-\widehat{\text{curl}}\alpha^{F}-\mbox{tr}\chi\sigma^{F}+\left(\eta-\underline{\eta}\right)\cdot\prescript{*}{}{\alpha}^{F},

(2.49)

\widehat{\nabla}_{3}\rho^{F}+\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}\rho^{F}=-\widehat{\text{div}}\underline{\alpha}^{F}+\left(\eta-\underline{\eta}\right)\cdot\underline{\alpha}^{F},

(2.50)

\widehat{\nabla}_{3}\sigma^{F}+\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}\sigma^{F}=-\widehat{\text{curl}}\underline{\alpha}^{F}+\left(\eta-\underline{\eta}\right)\cdot\prescript{*}{}{\underline{\alpha}}^{F}.

(2.51)

Remark 1
(An important renormalization)

As in
[
4
]
, the analysis will take place using the renormalized quantities
\beta^{R}
and
\underline{\beta}^{R}
introduced in (
2.14
). The benefit of using these, as also explained in
[
4
]
, is that the Bianchi equations can then be expressed in a way so
that the right-hand sides of the equations are directly expressible in terms of the Ricci coefficients
and the curvature and Yang-Mills components.

2.5
Integration

Let
U
be a coordinate patch on a
2-
sphere
S_{u,\underline{u}}
and let
p_{U}
be a partition of unity subordinate to
U
. For a function
\phi
, we define its integral on a
2-
sphere as well as on the null hypersurfaces
H_{u}
and
\underline{H}_{\underline{u}}
.

\int_{S_{u,\underline{u}}}\phi:=\sum_{U}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\phi\cdot p_{U}\cdot\sqrt{\text{det}\gamma}\hskip 1.42262pt\text{d}\theta^{1}\text{d}\theta^{2},

(2.52)

\int_{H_{u}^{(0,\underline{u})}}:=\sum_{U}\int_{0}^{\underline{u}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\phi\cdot 2\hskip 1.42262ptp_{U}\cdot\Omega\cdot\sqrt{\text{det}\gamma}\hskip 1.42262pt\text{d}\theta^{1}\text{d}\theta^{2}\hskip 1.42262pt\text{d}\underline{u}^{\prime},

(2.53)

\int_{\underline{H}_{\underline{u}}^{(u_{\infty},u)}}:=\sum_{U}\int_{u_{\infty}}^{u}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\phi\cdot 2\hskip 1.42262ptp_{U}\cdot\Omega\cdot\sqrt{\text{det}g}\hskip 1.42262pt\text{d}\theta^{1}\text{d}\theta^{2}\hskip 1.42262pt\text{d}u^{\prime},

(2.54)

For a spacetime region
\mathcal{D}_{u,\underline{u}}:=\begin{Bmatrix}\left(u^{\prime},\underline{u}^{\prime},\theta^{1},\theta^{2}\right)\hskip 1.42262pt\mid\hskip 1.42262ptu_{\infty}\leq u^{\prime}\leq u,0\leq\underline{u}^{\prime}\leq\underline{u}\end{Bmatrix}
, we define the spacetime integral

\int_{\mathcal{D}_{u,\underline{u}}}\phi:=\sum_{U}\int_{u_{\infty}}^{u}\int_{0}^{\underline{u}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\phi\cdot p_{U}\cdot\Omega^{2}\cdot\sqrt{-\text{det}g}\hskip 1.42262pt\text{d}\theta^{1}\text{d}\theta^{2}\hskip 1.42262pt\text{d}\underline{u}^{\prime}\hskip 1.42262pt\text{d}u^{\prime}.

(2.55)

We proceed with the definition of
L^{p}
norms
(1\leq p<\infty)
for an arbitrary tensorfield
\phi
:

\lVert\phi\rVert_{L^{p}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}^{p}:=\int_{S_{u,\underline{u}}}\langle\phi,\phi\rangle_{\gamma}^{\frac{p}{2}}

(2.56)

\lVert\phi\rVert_{L^{p}(H_{u}^{(0,\underline{u})})}^{p}:=\int_{H_{u}^{(0,\underline{u})}}\langle\phi,\phi\rangle_{\gamma}^{\frac{p}{2}}

(2.57)

\lVert\phi\rVert_{L^{p}(\underline{H}_{\underline{u}}^{(u_{\infty},u)})}^{p}:=\int_{\underline{H}_{\underline{u}}^{(u_{\infty},u)}}\langle\phi,\phi\rangle_{\gamma}^{\frac{p}{2}}.

(2.58)

For the case
p=\infty
, we separately define

\lVert\phi\rVert_{L^{\infty}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}:=\underset{(\theta^{1},\theta^{2})\in S_{u,\underline{u}}}{\text{sup}}\langle\phi,\phi\rangle_{\gamma}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\theta^{1},\theta^{2}).

(2.59)

2.6
Signature for decay rates and scale-invariant norms

Perhaps the most challenging aspect of trapped surface formation results, historically, has been the attempt to find initial data that are, in an appropriate sense, large (this is by necessity, as is implied by the monumental work of
[
11
]
) but also small enough to allow for an existence result of a spacetime region that gives trapped surfaces the time they would require to form. The first such initial data set, in the absence of symmetries, was given by
[
9
]
. Later contributions include
[
12
]
,
[
5
]
and
[
2
]
. Moreover, one would have to construct norms which preserve, at least approximately, the hierarchy present in the initial data upon evolution of the Einstein equations. The signature for decay rates, which was first introduced in
[
3
]
, is the tool we will use in the present paper to build
scale-invariant norms
. These will be norms that, upon evolution of the initial data, remain bounded above by a uniform constant (with the exception of a few anomalous terms). For another application of this framework, see
[
4
]
.

To each
\phi\in\begin{Bmatrix}\alpha,\underline{\alpha},\beta^{R},\underline{\beta}^{R},\rho,\sigma,\eta,\underline{\eta},\chi,\underline{\chi},\omega,\underline{\omega},\zeta,\gamma\end{Bmatrix}
we associate its
signature for decay rates

s_{2}(\phi)
:

s_{2}(\phi)=0\cdot N_{4}(\phi)+\frac{1}{2}N_{A}(\phi)+1\cdot N_{3}(\phi)-1.

Here
N_{\alpha}(\phi)

(\alpha=1,2,3,4)
denotes the number of times
e_{\alpha}
appears in the definition of
\phi
. We also extend the definition of
s_{2}
in the same way to the set of null components of the curvature
2-
form
F
, which we will schematically denote by
\mathcal{Y}
. We get the following tables of signatures:

\phi

\alpha

\underline{\alpha}

\beta^{R}

\underline{\beta}^{R}

\rho

\sigma

\eta

\underline{\eta}

\chi

\underline{\chi}

\omega

\underline{\omega}

\zeta

\gamma

s_{2}(\phi)

0

2

0.5

1.5

1

1

0.5

0.5

0

1

0

1

0.5

0

\mathcal{Y}

\alpha^{F}

\underline{\alpha}^{F}

\rho^{F}

\sigma^{F}

s_{2}(\mathcal{Y})

0

1

0.5

0.5

Several properties of
s_{2}
follow:

s_{2}(\nabla_{4}\phi)=s_{2}(\phi),\hskip 5.69054pts_{2}(\widehat{\nabla}_{4}\mathcal{Y})=s_{2}(\mathcal{Y}),

s_{2}(\nabla\phi)=s_{2}(\phi)+\frac{1}{2},\hskip 5.69054pts_{2}(\widehat{\nabla}\mathcal{Y})=s_{2}(\mathcal{Y})+\frac{1}{2},

s_{2}(\nabla_{3}\phi)=s_{2}(\phi)+1,\hskip 5.69054pts_{2}(\widehat{\nabla}_{3}\mathcal{Y})=s_{2}(\mathcal{Y})+1.

Finally, perhaps the most important property of
s_{2}
is
signature conservation
:

s_{2}(\phi_{1}\cdot\phi_{2})=s_{2}(\phi_{1})+s_{2}(\phi_{2}),\hskip 5.69054pts_{2}(\phi\cdot\mathcal{Y})=s_{2}(\phi)+s_{2}(\mathcal{Y})\hskip 5.69054pt\text{and}\hskip 5.69054pts_{2}(\mathcal{Y}_{1}\cdot\mathcal{Y}_{2})=s_{2}(\mathcal{Y}_{1})+s_{2}(\mathcal{Y}_{2}).

(2.60)

This allows for the (almost)-preservation of the scale-invariant norms upon evolution, as we shall see.

For any horizontal tensor-field
\phi
and Yang-Mills component
\mathcal{Y}
, we define the following norms:

\lVert{\phi\vee\mathcal{Y}}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{(sc)}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}:=a^{-s_{2}(\phi\vee\mathcal{Y})}\lvert u\rvert^{2s_{2}(\phi\vee\mathcal{Y})+1}\lVert{\phi\vee\mathcal{Y}}\rVert_{L^{\infty}(S_{u,\underline{u}})},

(2.61)

\lVert{\phi\vee\mathcal{Y}}\rVert_{L^{2}_{(sc)}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}:=a^{-s_{2}(\phi\vee\mathcal{Y})}\lvert u\rvert^{2s_{2}(\phi\vee\mathcal{Y})}\lVert{\phi\vee\mathcal{Y}}\rVert_{L^{2}(S_{u,\underline{u}})},

(2.62)

\lVert{\phi\vee\mathcal{Y}}\rVert_{L^{1}_{(sc)}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}:=a^{-s_{2}(\phi\vee\mathcal{Y})}\lvert u\rvert^{2s_{2}(\phi\vee\mathcal{Y})-1}\lVert{\phi\vee\mathcal{Y}}\rVert_{L^{1}(S_{u,\underline{u}})},

(2.63)

Notice the difference in the
u
-weights amongst the definitions.

A crucial property of the above norms is the
scale-invariant Hölder’s inequalities
that they satisfy. For
\mathrm{\Upsilon}
denoting an arbitrary
\phi
or
\mathcal{Y}
, there hold:

\lVert{\mathrm{\Upsilon}_{1}\cdot\mathrm{\Upsilon}_{2}}\rVert_{L^{1}_{(sc)}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}\leq\frac{1}{\lvert u\rvert}\lVert{\mathrm{\Upsilon}_{1}}\rVert_{L^{2}_{(sc)}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}\lVert{\mathrm{\Upsilon}_{2}}\rVert_{L^{2}_{(sc)}(S_{u,\underline{u}})},

(2.64)

\lVert{\mathrm{\Upsilon}_{1}\cdot\mathrm{\Upsilon}_{2}}\rVert_{L^{1}_{(sc)}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}\leq\frac{1}{\lvert u\rvert}\lVert{\mathrm{\Upsilon}_{1}}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{(sc)}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}\lVert{\mathrm{\Upsilon}_{2}}\rVert_{L^{1}_{(sc)}(S_{u,\underline{u}})},

(2.65)

\lVert{\mathrm{\Upsilon}_{1}\cdot\mathrm{\Upsilon}_{2}}\rVert_{L^{2}_{(sc)}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}\leq\frac{1}{\lvert u\rvert}\lVert{\mathrm{\Upsilon}_{1}}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{(sc)}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}\lVert{\mathrm{\Upsilon}_{2}}\rVert_{L^{2}_{(sc)}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}.

(2.66)

Notice that this is possible partly thanks to the signature conservation property (
2.60
). In the region of study, the factor
\frac{1}{\lvert u\rvert}
plays the role that
\delta^{\frac{1}{2}}
plays in the definition of the corresponding norms
7
7

7

See Section 2.17 in [Kl-Rod].
in [Kl-Rod], namely that of measuring the
smallness
of the nonlinear terms. The above inequalities are the primary tools that will be used to close the bootstrap argument required for the existence part.

2.7
Norms

Let
N\geq 3
be a natural number. Let
\psi_{g}\in\begin{Bmatrix}\omega,\mbox{tr}\chi,\underline{\omega},\eta,\underline{\eta}\end{Bmatrix}
,
\Psi_{u}\in\begin{Bmatrix}\beta^{R},\rho,\sigma,\underline{\beta}^{R}\end{Bmatrix}
and
\Psi_{\underline{u}}\in\begin{Bmatrix}\rho,\sigma,\underline{\beta}^{R},\underline{\alpha}\end{Bmatrix}
. Moreover, we will sometimes use
\Psi
to denote an arbitrary
\Psi_{u}
or a
\Psi_{\underline{u}}
. Also, define
\widetilde{\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}}:=\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}+\frac{2}{\lvert u\rvert}
and let
\mathcal{Y}_{\underline{u}}\in\begin{Bmatrix}\rho^{F},\sigma^{F},\underline{\alpha}^{F}\end{Bmatrix}
. For
0\leq i\leq N
, we define

\begin{split}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}}_{i,\infty}(u,\underline{u}):=\frac{1}{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}\lVert{(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla)^{i}\hat{\chi}}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{(sc)}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}&+\lVert{(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla)^{i}\psi_{g}}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{(sc)}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}+\frac{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\lvert u\rvert}\lVert{(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla)^{i}\underline{\hat{\chi}}}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{(sc)}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}\\
&+\frac{a}{\lvert u\rvert^{2}}\lVert{(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla)^{i}\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{(sc)}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}+\frac{a}{\lvert u\rvert}\lVert{(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla)^{i}\widetilde{\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}}}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{(sc)}(S_{u,\underline{u}})},\end{split}

(2.67)

\mathcal{R}_{i,\infty}(u,\underline{u}):=\frac{1}{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}\lVert{(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla)^{i}\alpha}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{(sc)}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}+\lVert{(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla)^{i}\Psi_{\underline{u}}}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{(sc)}(S_{u,\underline{u}})},

(2.68)

\begin{split}\mathbb{YM}_{\hskip 1.42262pti,\infty}(u,\underline{u}):=&\frac{1}{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}\lVert{(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\widehat{\nabla})^{i}\alpha_{F}}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{(sc)}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}+\lVert{(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\widehat{\nabla})^{i}\mathcal{Y}_{\underline{u}}}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{(sc)}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}.\end{split}

(2.69)

For
0\leq i\leq N-1
, define

\mathbb{YM}^{\hskip 1.42262pt\mathcal{C}}_{j,\infty}(u,\underline{u}):=\frac{1}{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}\lVert{(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\widehat{\nabla})^{i}\widehat{\nabla}_{4}\alpha^{F}}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{(sc)}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}+\frac{a}{\lvert u\rvert}\lVert{(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\widehat{\nabla})^{i}\widehat{\nabla}_{3}\underline{\alpha}^{F}}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{(sc)}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}.

(2.70)

Furthermore, for
0\leq i\leq N+3
and
0\leq j\leq N+4
, we define

\begin{split}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}}_{j,2}(u,\underline{u}):=\frac{1}{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}\lVert{(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla)^{i}\hat{\chi}}\rVert_{L^{2}_{(sc)}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}&+\lVert{(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla)^{i}\psi_{g}}\rVert_{L^{2}_{(sc)}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}+\frac{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\lvert u\rvert}\lVert{(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla)^{i}\underline{\hat{\chi}}}\rVert_{L^{2}_{(sc)}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}\\
&+\frac{a}{\lvert u\rvert^{2}}\lVert{(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla)^{i}\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}}\rVert_{L^{2}_{(sc)}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}+\frac{a}{\lvert u\rvert}\lVert{(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla)^{i}\widetilde{\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}}}\rVert_{L^{2}_{(sc)}(S_{u,\underline{u}})},\end{split}

(2.71)

\mathcal{R}_{i,2}(u,\underline{u}):=\frac{1}{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}\lVert{(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla)^{i}\alpha}\rVert_{L^{2}_{(sc)}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}+\lVert{(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla)^{i}\Psi_{\underline{u}}}\rVert_{L^{2}_{(sc)}(S_{u,\underline{u}})},

(2.72)

\mathbb{YM}_{\hskip 1.42262ptj,2}(u,\underline{u}):=\frac{1}{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}\lVert{(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\widehat{\nabla})^{j}\alpha_{F}}\rVert_{L^{2}_{(sc)}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}+\lVert{(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\widehat{\nabla})^{j}\mathcal{Y}_{\underline{u}}}\rVert_{L^{2}_{(sc)}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}.

(2.73)

For
0\leq j\leq N+2
, define

\mathbb{YM}^{\hskip 1.42262pt\mathcal{C}}_{j,2}(u,\underline{u}):=\frac{1}{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}\lVert{(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\widehat{\nabla})^{i}\widehat{\nabla}_{4}\alpha^{F}}\rVert_{L^{2}_{(sc)}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}+\frac{a}{\lvert u\rvert}\lVert{(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\widehat{\nabla})^{i}\widehat{\nabla}_{3}\underline{\alpha}^{F}}\rVert_{L^{2}_{(sc)}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}.

(2.74)

Finally, for
0\leq i\leq N+4
and
0\leq j\leq N+5
, we define the norms along the null hypersurfaces:

\mathcal{R}_{i}(u,\underline{u}):=\frac{1}{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}\lVert{(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla)^{i}\alpha}\rVert_{L^{2}_{(sc)}(H_{u}^{(0,\underline{u})})}+\lVert{(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla)^{i}\Psi_{u}}\rVert_{L^{2}_{(sc)}(H_{u}^{(0,\underline{u})})},

(2.75)

\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{i}(u,\underline{u}):=\frac{1}{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}\lVert{(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla)^{i}\beta^{R}}\rVert_{L^{2}_{(sc)}(\underline{H}_{\underline{u}}^{(u_{\infty},u)})}+\lVert{(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla)^{i}\Psi_{\underline{u}}}\rVert_{L^{2}_{(sc)}(\underline{H}_{\underline{u}}^{(u_{\infty},u)})}

(2.76)

\mathbb{YM}^{\mathcal{C}}_{j}:=\frac{1}{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}\lVert{(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\widehat{\nabla})^{i}\widehat{\nabla}_{4}\alpha^{F}}\rVert_{L^{2}_{(sc)}(H_{u}^{(0,\underline{u})})},

(2.77)

\underline{\mathbb{YM}}^{\mathcal{C}}_{j}:=\int_{u_{\infty}}^{u}\frac{a^{3}}{\lvert u^{\prime}\rvert^{4}}\lVert{(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\widehat{\nabla})^{i}\widehat{\nabla}_{3}\underline{\alpha}^{F}}\rVert_{L^{2}_{(sc)}(S_{u^{\prime},\underline{u}^{\prime}})}^{2}\hskip 1.42262pt\text{d}u^{\prime},

(2.78)

\mathbb{YM}_{j}:=\frac{1}{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}\lVert{(a^{\frac{1}{2}})^{j-1}\widehat{\nabla}^{j}\alpha^{F}}\rVert_{L^{2}_{(sc)}(H_{u}^{(0,\underline{u})})}+\lVert{(a^{\frac{1}{2}})^{j-1}\widehat{\nabla}^{j}(\rho^{F},\sigma^{F})}\rVert_{L^{2}_{(sc)}(H_{u}^{(0,\underline{u})})}

(2.79)

\underline{\mathbb{YM}}_{j}:=\frac{1}{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}\lVert{(a^{\frac{1}{2}})^{j-1}\widehat{\nabla}^{j}(\rho^{F},\sigma^{F})}\rVert_{L^{2}_{(sc)}(\underline{H}_{\underline{u}}^{(u_{\infty},u)})}+\lVert{(a^{\frac{1}{2}})^{j-1}\widehat{\nabla}^{j}\underline{\alpha}^{F}}\rVert_{L^{2}_{(sc)}(\underline{H}_{\underline{u}}^{(u_{\infty},u)})}

(2.80)

We now set
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}}_{i,\infty},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}}_{i,2},\mathcal{R}_{i,\infty},\mathcal{R}_{i,2},\mathbb{YM}_{\hskip 1.42262pti,\infty},\mathbb{YM}_{\hskip 1.42262pti,2}
to be the suprema over
(u,\underline{u})
of the corresponding norms. Finally, we define the total norms

{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}}:=\sum_{0\leq i\leq N}\left({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}}_{i,\infty}+\mathcal{R}_{i,\infty}+\mathbb{YM}_{\hskip 1.42262pti,\infty}\right)+\sum_{0\leq i\leq N+3}\mathcal{R}_{i,2}+\sum_{0\leq j\leq N+4}\left({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}}_{j,2}+\mathbb{YM}_{j,2}\right)

(2.81)

\mathcal{R}:=\sum_{0\leq i\leq N+4}\mathcal{R}_{i}+\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{i},\hskip 5.69054pt\mathbb{YM}:=\sum_{0\leq j\leq N+5}\mathbb{YM}_{j}+\underline{\mathbb{YM}}_{j}.

(2.82)

In addition, we also define the following top-order total norm for connection coefficients, that is to be controlled by means of separate elliptic estimates in terms of
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}},\mathcal{R}
and
\mathbb{YM}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{N+5,2}:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|(a^{\frac{1}{2}})^{N+4}\nabla^{N+5}\widehat{\chi}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}_{sc}(H^{(0,\underline{u})}_{u})}+\|(a^{\frac{1}{2}})^{N+4}\nabla^{N+5}(\mbox{tr}\chi,\omega)\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}_{sc}(H^{(0,\underline{u})}_{u})}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\|(a^{\frac{1}{2}})^{N+4}\nabla^{N+5}\eta\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}_{sc}(\underline{H}^{(u_{\infty},u)}_{\underline{u}})}+\frac{a}{|u|}\|(a^{\frac{1}{2}})^{N+4}\nabla^{N+5}(\eta,\underline{\eta})\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}_{sc}(H^{(0,\underline{u})}_{u})}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\|(a^{\frac{1}{2}})^{N+4}\nabla^{N+5}\underline{\omega}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}_{sc}(\underline{H}^{(u_{\infty},u)}_{\underline{u}})}+\int_{u_{\infty}}^{u}\frac{a^{\frac{3}{2}}}{|u^{{}^{\prime}}|^{3}}\|(a^{\frac{1}{2}})^{N+4}\nabla^{N+5}\widehat{\underline{\chi}}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}_{sc}(S_{u^{{}^{\prime}},\underline{u}})}du^{{}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{u_{\infty}}^{u}\frac{a^{2}}{|u^{{}^{\prime}}|^{3}}\|(a^{\frac{1}{2}})^{N+4}\nabla^{N+5}\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}_{sc}(S_{u^{{}^{\prime}},\underline{u}})}du^{{}^{\prime}}.

(2.83)

2.8
Commutation Formulae

Use the definition of the gauge-covariant derivative (
2.3
) and project it onto the topological 2-sphere
S_{u,\underline{u}}
to yield

\displaystyle[\widehat{\nabla}_{4},\widehat{\nabla}_{B}]\mathcal{G}^{P}~_{QA_{1}A_{2}A_{3}\cdot\cdot\cdot\cdot A_{n}}=[\widehat{D}_{4},\widehat{D}_{B}]\mathcal{G}^{P}~_{QA_{1}A_{2}A_{3}\cdot\cdot\cdot\cdot A_{n}}+(\nabla_{B}\log\Omega)\widehat{\nabla}_{4}\mathcal{G}^{P}~_{QA_{1}A_{2}A_{3}\cdot\cdot\cdot\cdot A_{n}}

\displaystyle-\gamma^{CD}\chi_{BD}\widehat{\nabla}_{C}\mathcal{G}^{P}~_{QA_{1}A_{2}A_{3}\cdot\cdot\cdot\cdot A_{n}}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\gamma^{CD}\chi_{BD}\underline{\eta}_{A_{i}}\mathcal{G}^{P}~_{QA_{1}A_{2}A_{3}\cdot\cdot\hat{A}_{i}C\cdot\cdot A_{n}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\gamma^{CD}\chi_{A_{i}B}\underline{\eta}_{D}\mathcal{G}^{P}~_{QA_{1}A_{2}A_{3}\cdot\cdot\hat{A}_{i}C\cdot\cdot A_{n}}

\displaystyle[\widehat{D}_{4},\widehat{D}_{A}]\mathcal{G}^{P}~_{QA_{1}A_{2}\cdot\cdot\cdot\cdot A_{n}}=-\sum_{i}R(e_{C},e_{A_{i}},e_{4},e_{A})\mathcal{G}^{P}~_{QA_{1}\cdot\cdot\hat{A}_{i},\cdot\cdot A_{n}}+F^{P}~_{R4A}\mathcal{G}^{R}~_{QA_{1}A_{2}\cdot\cdot\cdot A_{n}}

\displaystyle-F^{R}~_{Q4A}\mathcal{G}^{P}~_{RA_{1}A_{2}\cdot\cdot\cdot A_{n}}+(\nabla_{A}\log\Omega)\widehat{\nabla}_{4}\mathcal{G}^{P}~_{QA_{1}A_{2}\cdot\cdot\cdot\cdot A_{n}}.

Notice that the last term is redundant since it already appears in the previous expression. We need to take care of the curvature terms.

\displaystyle[\widehat{\nabla}_{4},\widehat{\nabla}_{A}]\mathcal{G}\sim(\beta+\alpha^{F}\cdot(\rho^{F}+\sigma^{F}))\mathcal{G}+\alpha^{F}\mathcal{G}+(\eta+\underline{\eta})\widehat{\nabla}_{4}\mathcal{G}-\chi\widehat{\nabla}\mathcal{G}+\chi\underline{\eta}\mathcal{G}.

Notice that if
\mathcal{G}
is not a section of the vector bundle
~{}^{k}\otimes T^{*}S\otimes P_{Ad,\mathfrak{g}}
(
P_{AD,\mathfrak{g}}
is the vector bundle associated to
P
by the adjoint representation of the gauge group
G
on
\mathfrak{g}
), then
\alpha^{F}\mathcal{G}
does not appear and the gauge covariant derivatives are simply ordinary covariant derivatives. For higher order commutation, we have the following lemma:

Lemma 2.1

Suppose
\mathcal{G}
is a section of the product vector bundle
~{}^{k}\otimes T^{*}\mathbb{S}^{2}\otimes P_{Ad,\rho}
,
k\geq 1
, that satisfies
\widehat{\nabla}_{4}\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{F}_{1}
and
\widehat{\nabla}_{4}\widehat{\nabla}^{I}\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{F}^{I}_{1}
, then
\mathcal{F}^{I}_{1}
verifies the following schematic expression:

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}^{I}_{1}\sim&\sum_{J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}+J_{4}=I-1}\nabla^{J_{1}}(\eta+\underline{\eta})^{J_{2}}\nabla^{J_{3}}\beta\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{4}}\mathcal{G}\\
+&\sum_{J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}+J_{4}+J_{5}=I-1}\nabla^{J_{1}}(\eta+\underline{\eta})^{J_{2}}\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{3}}\alpha^{F}\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{4}}(\rho^{F},\sigma^{F})\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{5}}\mathcal{G}\\
+&\sum_{J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}+J_{4}=I-1}\nabla^{J_{1}}(\eta+\underline{\eta})^{J_{2}}\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{3}}\alpha^{F}\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{4}}\mathcal{G}+\sum_{J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}=I}\nabla^{J_{1}}(\eta+\underline{\eta})^{J_{2}}\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{3}}\mathcal{F}_{1}\\
+&\sum_{J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}+J_{4}=I}\nabla^{J_{1}}(\eta+\underline{\eta})^{J_{2}}\hat{\nabla}^{J_{3}}\chi\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{4}}\mathcal{G}.\end{split}

(2.84)

Similarly, for
\widehat{\nabla}_{3}\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{F}_{2}
, and
\widehat{\nabla}_{3}\widehat{\nabla}^{I}\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{F}^{I}_{2}
,

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}^{I}_{2}\sim&\sum_{J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}+J_{4}=I-1}\nabla^{J_{1}}(\eta+\underline{\eta})^{J_{2}}\nabla^{J_{3}}\underline{\beta}\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{4}}\mathcal{G}\\
+&\sum_{J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}+J_{4}+J_{5}=I-1}\nabla^{J_{1}}(\eta+\underline{\eta})^{J_{2}}\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{3}}\underline{\alpha}^{F}\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{4}}(\rho^{F},\sigma^{F})\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{5}}\mathcal{G}\\
+&\sum_{J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}+J_{4}=I-1}\nabla^{J_{1}}(\eta+\underline{\eta})^{J_{2}}\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{3}}\underline{\alpha}^{F}\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{4}}\mathcal{G}+\sum_{J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}=I}\nabla^{J_{1}}(\eta+\underline{\eta})^{J_{2}}\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{3}}\mathcal{F}_{2}\\
+&\sum_{J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}+J_{4}=I}\nabla^{J_{1}}(\eta+\underline{\eta})^{J_{2}}\hat{\nabla}^{J_{3}}\underline{\chi}\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{4}}\mathcal{G}.\end{split}

(2.85)

Proof.

For
I=1
, this identity is clearly satisfied due to the calculations above. Assume, it holds for
J=I-1
and show that it holds for
J=I
. We omit the proof and refer to
[
4
]
.

\square

Remark 2

In both of the expressions, one can replace
\beta
(
\underline{\beta}
resp.) by the re-normalized curvature
\beta^{R}_{A}:=\beta_{A}-\frac{1}{2}R_{4A}
(
\underline{\beta}^{R}_{A}:=\underline{\beta}_{A}+\frac{1}{2}R_{3A}
resp.) and remove the quadratic terms
\alpha^{F}(\rho^{F},\sigma^{F})
(
\underline{\alpha}^{F}(\rho^{F},\sigma^{F})
resp.). By moving the top derivatives of
\mathcal{G}
multiplied by
\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}
from the right hand side to the left hand side, one may also obtain

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}^{I}_{2}+\frac{I}{2}\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}\hat{\nabla}^{I}\mathcal{G}\sim&\sum_{J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}+J_{4}=I-1}\nabla^{J_{1}}(\eta+\underline{\eta})^{J_{2}}\nabla^{J_{3}}\underline{\beta}^{R}\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{4}}\mathcal{G}\\
+&\sum_{J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}+J_{4}=I-1}\nabla^{J_{1}}(\eta+\underline{\eta})^{J_{2}}\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{3}}\underline{\alpha}^{F}\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{4}}\mathcal{G}\\
+&\sum_{J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}=I}\nabla^{J_{1}}(\eta+\underline{\eta})^{J_{2}}\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{3}}\mathcal{F}_{2}\\
+&\sum_{J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}+J_{4}=I}\nabla^{J_{1}}(\eta+\underline{\eta})^{J_{2}}\hat{\nabla}^{J_{3}}\widehat{\underline{\chi}}\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{4}}\mathcal{G}\\
+&\sum_{J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}+J_{4}=I-1}\nabla^{J_{1}}(\eta+\underline{\eta})^{J_{2}+1}\hat{\nabla}^{J_{3}}\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{4}}\mathcal{G}.\end{split}

Finally, if one takes into account the identities

\beta^{R}_{A}=-(\text{div}\hat{\chi})_{A}+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{A}\mbox{tr}\chi-\frac{1}{2}\left((\eta-\underline{\eta})\cdot(\hat{\chi}-\frac{1}{2}\mbox{tr}\chi\gamma)\right)_{A}-\frac{1}{2}R_{4A},

\underline{\beta}^{R}_{A}=(\text{\mbox{div }}\underline{\hat{\chi}})_{A}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{A}\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}-\frac{1}{2}\left((\eta-\underline{\eta})\cdot(\underline{\hat{\chi}}-\frac{1}{2}\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}\gamma)\right)_{A}+\frac{1}{2}R_{3A},

we arrive at the following identities:

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}_{1}^{I}\sim&\sum_{J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}=I}\nabla^{J_{1}}(\eta+\underline{\eta})^{J_{2}}\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{3}}\mathcal{F}_{1}\\
&+\sum_{J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}+J_{4}=I}\nabla^{J_{1}}(\eta+\underline{\eta})^{J_{2}}\nabla^{J_{3}}(\hat{\chi},\mbox{tr}\chi)\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{4}}\mathcal{G}\\
&+\sum_{J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}+J_{4}=I-1}\nabla^{J_{1}}(\eta+\underline{\eta})^{J_{2}+1}\nabla^{J_{3}}(\hat{\chi},\mbox{tr}\chi)\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{4}}\mathcal{G}\\
&+\sum_{J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}+J_{4}+J_{5}=I-1}\nabla^{J_{1}}(\eta+\underline{\eta})^{J_{2}}\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{3}}\alpha^{F}\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{4}}(\rho^{F},\sigma^{F})\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{5}}\mathcal{G}\\
&+\underbrace{\sum_{J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}+J_{4}=I-1}\nabla^{J_{1}}(\eta+\underline{\eta})^{J_{2}}\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{3}}\alpha^{F}\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{4}}\mathcal{G}}_{C_{1}}\end{split}

(2.86)

and

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}_{2}^{I}+\frac{I}{2}\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}\widehat{\nabla}^{I}\mathcal{G}&\sim\sum_{J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}=I}\nabla^{J_{1}}(\eta+\underline{\eta})^{J_{2}}\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{3}}\mathcal{F}_{2}\\
&+\sum_{J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}+J_{4}=I}\nabla^{J_{1}}(\eta+\underline{\eta})^{J_{2}}\nabla^{J_{3}}(\underline{\hat{\chi}},\hskip 1.42262pt\widetilde{\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}})\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{4}}\mathcal{G}\\
&+\sum_{J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}+J_{4}=I-1}\nabla^{J_{1}}(\eta+\underline{\eta})^{J_{2}+1}\hat{\nabla}^{J_{3}}\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{4}}\mathcal{G}\\
&+\sum_{J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}+J_{4}=I-1}\nabla^{J_{1}}(\eta+\underline{\eta})^{J_{2}+1}\nabla^{J_{3}}(\underline{\hat{\chi}},\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi})\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{4}}\mathcal{G}\\
&+\sum_{J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}+J_{4}+J_{5}=I-1}\nabla^{J_{1}}(\eta+\underline{\eta})^{J_{2}}\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{3}}\underline{\alpha}^{F}\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{4}}(\rho^{F},\sigma^{F})\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{5}}\mathcal{G}\\
&+\underbrace{\sum_{J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}+J_{4}=I-1}\nabla^{J_{1}}(\eta+\underline{\eta})^{J_{2}}\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{3}}\underline{\alpha}^{F}\widehat{\nabla}^{J_{4}}\mathcal{G}}_{C_{2}}.\end{split}

(2.87)

Remark 3

Note that the commutation terms
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are produced because of the non-abelian (and hence non-linear) characteristic of the Yang-Mills theory. For any section of the bundle
\otimes^{k}T^{*}S_{u,\underline{u}}
,
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
drop out.

2.9
Gauge-invariant inequalities for scale-invariant norms

Let the space-time gauge covariant derivative be denoted by
\widehat{D}
and let its projection on the horizontal direction, i.e., on the topological
2-
sphere, be denoted by
\widehat{\nabla}
. More precisely
\widehat{D}_{\mu}:=D_{\mu}+[A_{\mu},\cdot]
and
\widehat{\nabla}_{A}:=\nabla_{A}+[A(e_{A}),\cdot]
, where
\nabla
is the horizontal component of the usual spacetime covariant derivative
D
. For
\mathcal{G}\in\Gamma(^{N}\otimes T^{*}S\otimes P_{AD,\mathfrak{g}})
, we define the gauge-invariant norm as follows:

\displaystyle|\mathcal{G}|^{2}_{\gamma,\delta}:=\mathcal{G}^{P}~_{QA_{1}A_{2}A_{3}\cdot\cdot\cdot\cdot A_{N}}\mathcal{G}^{Q}~_{PB_{1}B_{2}B_{3}\cdot\cdot\cdot\cdot B_{N}}\gamma^{A_{1}B_{1}}\gamma^{A_{2}B_{2}}\gamma^{A_{3}B_{3}}\cdot\cdot\cdot\cdot\gamma^{A_{N}B_{N}},

(2.88)

where repeated indices
P
and
Q
represent the inner product with respect to the metric
\delta
on the fibres of the vector bundle
P_{AD,\mathfrak{g}}
. Notice that due to the compatibility of the fibre metric
\delta
with the connection
\widehat{\nabla}
, we have the following

\displaystyle\nabla|\mathcal{G}|^{2}_{\gamma,\delta}=2\langle\widehat{\nabla}\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}\rangle_{\gamma,\delta},

(2.89)

where
\langle~,\rangle_{\gamma,\delta}
denotes the joint inner product induced by the metrics
\gamma
and
\delta
. Clearly
|\mathcal{G}|^{2}_{\gamma,\delta}=\langle\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}\rangle_{\gamma,\delta}
. Now we proceed to define the
L^{p}
(
1\leq p<\infty
) norms of an arbitrary section
\mathcal{G}\in\Gamma(^{N}\otimes T^{*}S\otimes P_{AD,\mathfrak{g}})
:

\displaystyle||\mathcal{G}||^{p}_{L^{p}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}:=\int_{S_{u,\underline{u}}}\langle\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}\rangle^{\frac{p}{2}}_{\gamma,\delta},~||\mathcal{G}||^{p}_{L^{p}(H_{u})}:=\int_{H_{u}}\langle\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}\rangle^{\frac{p}{2}}_{\gamma,\delta},~||\mathcal{G}||^{p}_{L^{p}(\underline{H}_{\underline{u}})}:=\int_{\underline{H}_{\underline{u}}}\langle\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}\rangle^{\frac{p}{2}}_{\gamma,\delta}.

(2.90)

In the case
p=\infty
, we define the
L^{\infty}
norm as follows:

\displaystyle||\mathcal{G}||_{L^{\infty}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}:=\sup_{\theta\in S_{u,\underline{u}}}\langle\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\gamma,\delta}.

(2.91)

In order to establish Sobolev inequalities on
S_{u,\underline{u}}
(note that these topological
2-
spheres are evolving in spacetime), we need to have estimates for the metric
\gamma
. These are carried out in Section
3
.

2.10
Yang-Mills pair integration

To close the energy estimates for curvature and thus establish semi-global existence, we ought to obtain energy estimates for the Yang-Mills fields. To that end, instead of using the Yang-Mills stress energy tensor to construct suitable currents, integrate over the bulk
\mathcal{D}_{u,\underline{u}}
, and control the error terms, we directly utilize the manifestly hyperbolic character of the null Yang-Mills equations. However, since the gauge covariant derivative appears in the equations instead of the ordinary covariant derivative, we need to be careful while performing a direct integration by parts procedure by means of the equations. As we shall see, we once again utilize the compatibility of the gauge covariant derivative with the metric on the fibres of the bundle
P_{AD,\mathfrak{g}}
. We briefly sketch the procedure here, even though we will perform the actual energy estimates later. First recall the following integration lemma:

Lemma 2.2

Let
f:\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}
be a gauge-invariant object on the spacetime. The following integration by parts identities hold for
f
:

\displaystyle\int_{D_{u,\underline{u}}}\nabla_{4}f=\int_{\underline{H}_{\underline{u}}}f-\int_{\underline{H}_{\underline{u}_{0}}}f+\int_{D_{u,\underline{u}}}(2\omega-\mbox{tr}\chi)f

(2.92)

and

\displaystyle\int_{D_{u,\underline{u}}}\nabla_{3}f=\int_{H_{u}}f-\int_{H_{u_{0}}}f+\int_{D_{u,\underline{u}}}(2\underline{\omega}-\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi})f,

(2.93)

Proof.

The proof is a simple consequence of the following integration by parts procedure:

\begin{split}\int_{D_{u\underline{u}}}\nabla_{4}f=&\int_{u_{0}}^{u}du^{{}^{\prime}}\int_{\underline{u}_{0}}^{\underline{u}}\left(\int_{S_{u^{{}^{\prime}}\underline{u}^{{}^{\prime}}}}\frac{\partial f}{\partial\underline{u}^{{}^{\prime}}}\Omega\mu_{\gamma}\right)d\underline{u}^{{}^{\prime}}\\
=&\int_{u_{0}}^{u}du^{{}^{\prime}}\int_{\underline{u}_{0}}^{\underline{u}}\left\{\frac{d}{d\underline{u}^{{}^{\prime}}}\int_{S_{u^{{}^{\prime}}\underline{u}^{{}^{\prime}}}}f\Omega\mu_{\gamma}-\int_{S_{u^{{}^{\prime}}\underline{u}^{{}^{\prime}}}}f(\frac{\partial\Omega}{\partial\underline{u}^{{}^{\prime}}}+\frac{\Omega}{2}\mbox{tr}_{\gamma}\partial_{u}\gamma)\mu_{\gamma}\right\}d\underline{u}^{{}^{\prime}}\\
=&\int_{u_{0}}^{u}du^{{}^{\prime}}\int_{S_{u^{{}^{\prime}}\underline{u}}}f\Omega\mu_{\gamma}-\int_{u_{0}}^{u}\int_{S_{u^{{}^{\prime}}\underline{u}_{0}}}f\Omega\mu_{\gamma}+\int_{D_{u\underline{u}}}f(2\omega-\mbox{tr}\chi)\\
=&\int_{\underline{H}_{\underline{u}}}f-\int_{\underline{H}_{\underline{u}_{0}}}f+\int_{D_{u\underline{u}}}(2\omega-\mbox{tr}\chi)f.\end{split}

The other part follows in a similar fashion.

\square

Proposition 2.1

Null Yang-Mills equations are manifestly hyperbolic.

Remark 4

Note that this is natural since the Yang-Mills equations are derivable from a Lagrangian and as such they are equipped with a canonical stress-energy tensor.

Proof.

The integration lemma can be utilized to prove the manifestly hyperbolic characteristics of the Yang-Mills equations while expressed in the double null coordinates. First consider the null triple
(\underline{\alpha}^{F},\rho^{F},\sigma^{F})
